# Testing

How Goodfetch is tested: every surface, at every level. Each rule comes with its reason, and the hard-to-reverse ones are decided in ADRs. [`STYLE.md`](STYLE.md) shapes the code these tests drive. Here a *snapshot* is a stored expected value, never a Checkpoint, a Group backup or a Reference screenshot.

## Levels and layout

A test's level is the seam it crosses, not its speed:

- A **unit test** tests one module through its interface, in one process.
- An **integration test** tests one package through its public interface, with real local stand-ins and real processes.
- An **end-to-end test** follows a Journey through a shipped surface.
- The **conformance suites** of the ADRs stay the interface tests of every contract with several implementations.

| | Unit tests | Integration tests |
|---|---|---|
| Rust crate | `#[cfg(test)] mod tests` at the end of the module file, or in `<module>/tests.rs` | `tests/it/main.rs` and its modules |
| TypeScript package, Console | `foo.test.ts` (or `.tsx`) beside `foo.ts` | `<package>/tests/`, importing only the package's own name |
| Python package | `tests/unit/`, mirroring the module paths under `src/` | `tests/integration/`, importing only the public package |

- **A unit test reaches only its module's interface:** the items the module's callers use. Never a private function.
  _Why:_ such tests survive internal refactors, and they test what callers rely on.
- **Product code has no `#[cfg(test)]` branch and no `pub` item added for tests.** If a test needs one, the module is the wrong shape. The one test-only `cfg` in product code is the `sync` modules' `goodfetch_loom` switch ([Concurrency](#concurrency)).
- **One integration-test binary per crate, run with `cargo-nextest`.** A crate splits it into named area harnesses (`tests/<area>/main.rs`, `autotests = false`) only when its pull request shows a localized test edit taking more than 5 seconds to rebuild with one harness.
  _Why:_ each file in `tests/` links its own binary. uv's measurement favours one harness until a suite is large.
- **Tests of a Product's executable live in its crate's `tests/it`** and find the executable through `CARGO_BIN_EXE_goodfetch-collie` or `CARGO_BIN_EXE_goodfetch-foxhound`, never a guessed path.
  _Why:_ the build directory's layout may change without notice.
- **Foxhound's tests live in Foxhound's crates.** No crate outside `crates/foxhound/` but `xtask` takes a Foxhound crate, even as a dev-dependency, and the workspace-wide `cargo-deny` check refuses one (ADR 0087).
  _Why:_ Collie's tests then build and pass without Foxhound's code.
- **No crate outside `crates/foxhound/` names `goodfetch-protocol`'s `foxhound` module** but `goodfetch-protocol` and `xtask`, and the check refuses a fixture crate that does, through a `use` or a full path ([ADR 0099](docs/adr/0099-every-surface-spells-each-catalog-name-by-its-own-rule-shapes-come-from-declared-kinds-and-call-is-the-way-down.md)).
  _Why:_ Foxhound's contract types sit in a crate Collie links, so cargo-deny alone cannot keep Collie's code from naming them (ADR 0087).
- **In Collie, only the TLS relay's crate takes BoringSSL** ([ADR 0124](docs/adr/0124-collies-tls-relay-links-wreqs-boringssl-but-not-wreq-and-forwards-the-browsers-own-http2.md)). Collie's `cargo-deny` check refuses `wreq` and the crates that exist for it under any parent. It refuses `tokio-btls` under any direct parent but the relay's crate, `btls` under any but the relay's crate and `tokio-btls`, and `btls-sys` under any but `btls` (and the relay's crate if it calls the FFI).
  _Why:_ the relay is Collie's one use of BoringSSL; any other would start moving Foxhound's impersonated HTTP into Collie.
- **The declared dependencies of the npm and PyPI packages under `sdks/`, `adapters/` and `integrations/` are checked in the pull-request tier** against `STYLE.md`'s package dependency rule (ADR 0095), as `cargo-deny` checks the crates.
  _Why:_ a Foxhound package depending on `@goodfetch/collie` would install Collie's executable with every Foxhound (ADR 0090).
- **Each Product's executable crate tests its composition** in the Quick suite, in process: exactly one handler for each operation of its Product's catalog, and Foxhound's composition contains all of Collie's (ADR 0087).
  _Why:_ the composition is a hand-kept list, and Foxhound must carry everything Collie carries.
- **Python tests run with `--import-mode=importlib` and `strict = true`** in `[tool.pytest]`.
  _Why:_ tests outside the package run against the installed wheel, as pytest recommends.

### Which Product's executable a test runs

Decided in [ADR 0094](docs/adr/0094-both-products-are-released-and-signed-together-and-each-test-runs-on-collie-unless-it-needs-foxhound.md).

- **A test that runs an executable runs Collie's when Collie carries everything it needs, and Foxhound's otherwise:** once, never once per Product. A test that needs Foxhound anywhere in its setup or steps, such as a rolling upgrade under a Partitioned crawl, runs on Foxhound's.
  _Why:_ Foxhound's executable links Collie's crates, so a test of Collie's covers the code users of either Product run, and the containment checks below cover what Foxhound adds around it.
- **A test that measures an executable itself runs on each Product's:** its size (ADR 0087), its startup benchmarks and its idle memory.
- **Each package is tested against its own Product's executable:** each executable crate's `tests/it` (its CLI, MCP server, exit codes and `--help` tree), and each Product's SDK packages, Console build, image and Helm chart.
- **One test build per target serves both Products:** the in-process tests and both executables come from the same workspace invocation. Never add a test build per Product.
  _Why:_ Cargo unifies features across the packages of one invocation, so Collie's executable in a test links the same shared code as Foxhound's, and a second build would compile again every crate whose features differ. Each Product's own `cargo build -p` (ADR 0087) is run from its own bytes by the release before it publishes.
- **Foxhound proves that it contains Collie at the seams where its executable is composed,** never by running Collie's tests again:
  1. its engine composition contains Collie's (ADR 0087's composition test);
  2. a Quick check fails unless every generated contract of Collie's (catalog entries, setting keys, Error codes, events, CLI commands with their flags, MCP tools) is in Foxhound's, the same;
  3. a served test on Foxhound's executable visits every route of Collie's Console build and checks that it renders;
  4. each Collie Journey's `cli` variant also runs on Foxhound's executable, as `J-NNN@foxhound-cli` ([Journeys](#journeys)).
- **A conformance suite runs on the smallest Product that carries its contract:** browser conformance and the Plugin kinds Collie carries on Collie's executable, Sinks and Fetch backends on Foxhound's. Of the [Session ownership and proxy limit cases](#sdks), those on a Session a program or an Agent opened and on a Collie Run's proxy limits run on Collie's, and the one on a crawl's Session pool on Foxhound's.

### The Cloud-readiness conformance suite

Decided in [ADR 0068](docs/adr/0068-the-console-is-tested-in-real-browsers-and-served-by-the-real-binary.md) and [ADR 0096](docs/adr/0096-the-browser-host-runs-from-either-products-executable-and-goodfetch-cloud-runs-each-run-on-the-product-it-needs.md) (spec #586). Every contract Goodfetch Cloud relies on is a case of this suite, `conformance/cloud-readiness/cases.toml`, with the messages it expects; no prose document stands beside it, and a new cloud-facing contract lands with its case, and its line below, in the same pull request. `goodfetch-conformance` runs it against two kinds of target, each capability naming its kind (`target`), and writes one `conformance` document per target:

- **A gateway, reached by address** (`GatewayTarget`: its `http` or `https` origin, `GatewayAddress`, whose `/protocol` WebSocket is `ws` or `wss`; the token a program presents in its `hello`, as spec #586's duty 8 says; and any certificate its TLS chains to besides Mozilla's roots): the Stub gateway here, plain and behind a TLS terminator, Goodfetch Cloud's gateway from its private repository, the cases unchanged (`GatewaySuite::embedded`, `run_gateway_suite`). Each case opens its own Connections as the Clients it names, a Collie or Foxhound Client (surface `rust`), a model's Client (surface `mcp`) or the Console, and tells which engine a run landed on from what that engine answers a Foxhound Client's `run.seeds`: an answer from Foxhound's, `not_in_product` naming Collie as the run's Product and no `install` command from Collie's, which words it so only behind the gateway's mark (ADR 0095), on every channel. `goodfetch-stub-gateway`'s `tests/it` runs it against the Stub gateway, keeping `conformance-cloud-readiness-gateway.json`.
- **An engine driven as a gateway drives one:** its cases join with their tickets.

The gateway cases, with their `covers`:

- `a-collie-client-and-a-foxhound-client-are-each-answered-as-their-product` (`story:14-14`, `story:14-15`): a Collie Client's `hello` is answered as Collie; a Foxhound Client's as Foxhound, or with `not_in_product` by a gateway that runs no Foxhound engine (the Stub gateway given no `--foxhound`).
- `a-client-of-another-protocol-version-is-refused-at-hello` (`story:14-14`): a Client's `hello` of the next protocol minor answers `protocol_version_unsupported`, the Daemon's version rule (ADR 0066).
- `a-models-client-is-held-to-a-models-settings-behind-the-gateway` (`story:14-14`): a model's Client meets the rules it meets locally (ADR 0092), the gateway naming its surface to the engine (`client_surface`): `proxy.server` on its run and on its Session answers `setting_locked`.
- `a-collie-clients-run-lands-on-collies-engine` (`story:14-14`): ADR 0096's row for a Client, needing Collie.
- `a-foxhound-clients-run-lands-on-foxhounds-engine` (`story:14-15`): the same row, needing Foxhound.
- `a-console-run-naming-nothing-only-foxhound-carries-lands-on-collies-engine` (`story:14-17`): the row for the Console with no Recipe, a target naming nothing of Foxhound's.
- `a-console-crawl-start-lands-on-foxhounds-engine` (`story:14-17`): the same row, a target naming an operation only Foxhound carries.
- `a-console-run-setting-a-crawl-key-lands-on-foxhounds-engine` (`story:14-17`): the same row, a setting key only Foxhound carries, which Foxhound's engine answers with `setting_not_applicable` on a run that is no crawl.
- `a-start-naming-a-known-run-id-goes-to-that-runs-engine` (`story:14-17`): a start naming a Run ID the gateway's index holds stays on that run's engine, which answers it with `run_conflict` for its other inputs.
- `a-collie-programs-raw-crawl-start-answers-not-in-product-naming-the-runs-product` (`story:14-16`): a Collie Client's `crawl.start` answers `not_in_product` from Collie's engine, naming the run's Product and no install.
- `a-collie-programs-crawl-setting-answers-not-in-product-naming-the-runs-product` (`story:14-16`): the same for a crawl setting key in a Collie Client's `run.start`.
- `a-collie-programs-crawl-setting-in-a-sessions-settings-answers-not-in-product-naming-the-runs-product` (`story:14-16`): the same for a crawl setting key in the settings of a Session the Collie Client opens on its run, as for every other call that reads a Client's settings.
- `a-collie-client-reads-a-foxhound-run-it-did-not-start` (`story:14-20`): a Collie Client reads a Foxhound Client's run's `run.usage` and `run.seeds`, which Foxhound's engine answers: an engine never narrows its catalog for a Client (ADRs 0095 and 0096), and a call the run's engine lacks is the next case's.
- `a-foxhound-clients-call-a-collie-runs-engine-lacks-answers-not-in-product` (`story:14-20`): a Foxhound Client's `run.seeds` on a Collie Client's run answers `not_in_product` from Collie's engine.

The routing table's Recipe rows (a Recipe that names Foxhound, a Recipe that names Collie held to Collie) join once a start carries a Recipe's manifest.

## Writing a test

- **One behaviour per test, through the interface.** Several assertions are fine when they check that one behaviour.
- **Arrange, act, assert, in that order, with one act.**
- **No branch or loop computes an expected value.** Expected values come from a literal, the spec or the Proving ground's oracle.
  _Why:_ an expected value computed the way the code computes it can never disagree with the code.
- **Assert a failure on its Error code, never on its message text.**
  _Why:_ Error codes are the contract (ADR 0038); messages change freely.
- **Every test creates its own data.** No test reads another test's data or depends on test order.
- **Names state the behaviour in glossary terms**, never `_Avoid_` terms:
  - Rust: `resumed_run_never_repeats_an_effect`, with no `test_` prefix.
  - Python: `test_resumed_run_never_repeats_an_effect`.
  - TypeScript: `describe("<interface under test>")` with `test("resumed run never repeats an Effect")`, at most two `describe` levels.
  - No `should`, no issue numbers, no `it_works`.
  - Integration-test modules and files are named by behaviour area (`tests/it/resume.rs`), not by source file.
- **Every case of a parameterized test has a name:** rstest's `#[case::name]` in Rust, Vitest's `test.for` with the name in the title, pytest's `parametrize` with an `id` per case. Never loop over cases inside one test.
  _Why:_ a loop stops at the first failing case and hides the others; rstest makes each case its own nextest test.
- **No mocking of owned code:**
  - `cargo-deny` denies `mockall`, `mockito`, `wiremock`, `faux` and `mry`;
  - oxlint's `vitest/no-restricted-vi-methods` refuses at least `vi.mock`, `vi.doMock`, `vi.importMock`, `vi.mockObject`, `vi.spyOn`, `vi.stubGlobal` and `vi.stubEnv`;
  - ruff's `TID251` bans `unittest.mock`, `pytest.MonkeyPatch` and `_pytest.monkeypatch`, the module `pytest.MonkeyPatch` is imported from;
  - pytest-mock is in no dependency group (CI checks `uv.lock`), and the test-support pytest plugin fails the collection of any test that requests `monkeypatch` or `mocker`, directly or through another fixture. A third-party plugin fixture that uses `monkeypatch` therefore cannot be adopted.

  _Why:_ a mocked collaborator ties the test to the implementation.
- **The TypeScript and Python SDKs read their environment from a map an option can replace**, defaulting to the process's own. Tests pass the map instead of patching the environment.
  _Why:_ a patched environment leaks between tests and hides what a call depends on.
- **A test is never retried.** A flaky test is quarantined the same day, with an issue, and blocks its milestone and every release ([Flaky tests and Live checks](#flaky-tests-and-live-checks)).
  _Why:_ a retry turns a real race into a green run, and people stop trusting red ones.

## Dependencies in tests

A module is tested according to the kind of dependency it has, using the four categories of `codebase-design`:

- **In-process** (pure logic: URL normalization, robots, frontier rules, extraction, run-state rules): through the module's interface; `proptest` for parsers.
- **Local-substitutable** (a real local stand-in exists): SQLite in a temporary file; a real Chromium (the Goodfetch build of ADR 0035) against Proving ground pages served locally. CDP is never mocked.
  _Why:_ a fake browser lets tests pass where a real one fails, which is the false success ADR 0025 measures.
- **Remote but owned** (Goodfetch's own processes across a boundary: sink processes, process Plugins, the Daemon): the logic is tested through an in-memory adapter of the protocol and, for several hosts, in the [Simulation](#simulation), and the real processes through the conformance suites.
- **True external** (model providers, CAPTCHA services): a stand-in adapter. For Model providers, the Scripted model in-process, Model recordings replayed by `httpmock` at the adapter's base URL (matched on the body, secrets removed by an allowlist), and the Proving ground's Fake model end to end; no test calls a live model ([AI features and agent skills](#ai-features-and-agent-skills), ADR 0065).
- **An `httpmock` test binds a socket, so it sits in `tests/it`**, never among the unit tests ([The three suites](#the-three-suites)).

How each dependency is injected is in [Injection and determinism](#injection-and-determinism).

## Shared helpers

- **Rust helpers live in `goodfetch-test-support`** (`crates/goodfetch-test-support`, `publish = false`): the per-test folder, `GOODFETCH_HOME` and cleared environment, the printed seed, unique names, reported addresses, the Proving ground started from a seed, commands with the cleared environment, and the shared snapshot redactions and filters.
  - A test starts with `TestEnv::new()`: its folder (kept and listed as `goodfetch-test-folder-kept=<path>` when the test fails), `home()`, the cleared `env()` map, `seed()` (printed as `goodfetch-test-seed=<n>`), `start_time()` (2026-01-05T09:00:00Z), `unique_name(prefix)` and `suite()`; `.with_variable(name, value)` adds a variable to the map every child gets. `TestEnv::in_memory()` makes the same setup with its folder on a memory file system (`/dev/shm` on Linux, the temporary folder elsewhere), for an in-process test of a store whose cases each write a store of their own: on a busy disk every sync and new file waits for the other writers' data, and the Audit log's property tests passed the Quick kill that way (fix-queue 123). The Daemon soak is the one engine test on it: its HTTP crawls start no browser, and a busy disk slowed its Runs' stores past its time cap (fix-queue 128). Any other test that starts a browser or an engine keeps `new()`, whose copies would sit in memory. A failed in-memory test's folder is kept on disk, copied to the temporary folder under its own name and listed there, and removed from memory, where every kept folder would hold RAM until someone deleted it; a test killed on its timeout runs no teardown, so the next in-memory setup moves each folder whose process is gone (its name carries the process ID) and lists it as `goodfetch-test-folder-moved=<path>`. Its home starts with a user configuration holding `TEST_SETTINGS` and the test's `log.level`, so the Console of every Daemon the test starts listens on port 0, never on its major's own port (ADR 0059), and every engine process writes its JSON log (`log.output = "file"`); `user_configuration(settings)` writes it again with the test's own settings after them, each default left out whose key the settings set; `log_level(filter)` sets `log.level` in the file as it is, whoever wrote it (`info` keeps the trace lines, which carry their instant, out of two pseudo-terminal runs a colour property compares; `trace` makes any log line on stdout fail a `StrictStdout` test). It starts children through `command(&env, program)` or `env.spawn(…)`, and its teardown fails the test when one is still alive. Its user configuration turns on the JSON log of every engine the test starts, into the home's `logs/` folder (ADR 0122), and under `just test pre-merge` or `release` its teardown copies those lines, its command record ([CLI](#cli)) and its answer record ([Feature coverage](#feature-coverage)) into the test's exercise record, which the exercise audit reads; an in-process test that installs `goodfetch_test_support::subscriber` gets its dispatch and outcome records there too ([Feature coverage](#feature-coverage)). Under `just coverage` the map also passes on `LLVM_PROFILE_FILE`, so each instrumented executable writes its coverage profile.
  - **Every test has a crash-dump folder**, `crash_dump_folder()` (`<home>/crash-dumps`), which the map names in Chromium's own `BREAKPAD_DUMP_LOCATION` (`CRASH_DUMP_VARIABLE`), so every browser a test launches writes its crash dumps there. Teardown fails the test when the folder or a subfolder holds any file but the `settings.dat` Chromium's crash reporter keeps at its root whenever it runs, with a `CrashDumps` payload listing each one, and keeps the folder ([ADR 0074](docs/adr/0074-the-goodfetch-chromium-is-gated-where-it-is-built-against-a-control-build-of-the-same-revision.md)). `goodfetch-test-env` exits non-zero at teardown for the same reason, which the Vitest fixtures report as `teardown_failed`.
  - **Tests find the commit's executables through the test tool manifest**, `tools()`: `just test` builds each one the workspace has (both Products', the Proving ground's, `goodfetch-test-env`, `goodfetch-scripted-engine` and `goodfetch-conformance`, whose `read <file>` loads a result document through `goodfetch-protocol`'s reader, and `read <file> --journey <Journey file>` also compares a `journey` document with its Journey) with the tests' Cargo profile, through `xtask test-tools`, and puts the manifest's path in `GOODFETCH_TEST_TOOLS` and the suite in `GOODFETCH_TEST_SUITE`. Run outside `just test`, a test that needs one fails, naming `just test`.
  - **A test that drives Proving ground pages starts its own Proving ground**, `ProvingGround::start(&env, seed)`: the tool manifest's Proving ground runs `serve` on port 0 of loopback under the test's environment, its process joins the test's record, and it stops when the value drops: interrupted with `SIGINT`, as `stop()` does, so it ends its connections and undoes any network shaping, and killed only when it outlasts its 10 s deadline. `stop()` answers its exit status. A binary that prints no first line within `PROVING_GROUND_DEADLINE` (30 s) fails with `ProvingGroundError::Line`. `description()` holds its `address`, `version` and `seed`, `resolve` (every host name it serves, mapped to the address's IP, for the `resolve` setting), `ca` (its certificate authority's PEM file in the test's folder) and `fixtures`: the forward `proxies` by name with their `proxy_credentials`, the `dns` fixture, the watched `target` and `connections`, the connection record's URL. `url(host, path)` gives a page's URL on the bound port, `start_url(id)` the start URL of a task of its task list, such as `actionability/2`, and `judge(id, status)` the oracle's verdict on that task as a tool reports it (`success` or `failure`), from the Effects its pages recorded. `ProvingGround::start_program(&env, path, seed, deadline)` starts another executable, such as an old tag's.
  - **A test counts the requests a site received through `ReceivedRequests`**, never the observations' raw `requests` list: `proving_ground.received_requests()`, or `ReceivedRequests::of(&observations)` for observations read once. `of_the_case()` leaves out `OWN_TARGETS`, the robots.txt the engine reads of each loaded document's origin, which nothing a case calls waits for ([ADR 0086](docs/adr/0086-a-crawl-obeys-robots-txt-and-sends-one-request-at-a-time-per-domain-by-default-and-pressing-harder-is-recorded.md)), and the icon Chromium asks a site for on its own. `every()` keeps them, for a claim about all a site saw (an exact list, "nothing more", each request's headers, a robots.txt test); where order matters, its count comes after a named state, `robots_txt_read(&env, run_id)`, which returns once the Run's Provenance records a robots.txt reading, so the read's request has reached the site. The conformance harness's `ProvingGround::received_requests()` gives its cases the same. `xtask received-requests`, in `just lint`, refuses any other read of the list in a file under `crates/` that reads the observations.
    _Why:_ both land before or after any count a test takes, so a count over every request is a race under load ([ADR 0064](docs/adr/0064-tests-are-never-retried-and-unpinned-checks-run-as-live-checks.md)); five counts failed that way.
  - **A test reads where the Proving ground's DNS fixture was asked from through `ProvingGround::dns_query_sources(&observations, name)`**: the source address of each query for `name`, in arrival order.
  - **A test that launches a browser takes its Browser cache from `browser_cache(&env)`**: a cache in the test's folder that holds the Chrome for Testing build Collie pins (`CHROME_FOR_TESTING_BUILD`), laid out as a prepared copy holds it, its files hard links to (or, across file systems, copies of) the same build Playwright installs for the repository's own browser tests (`PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH`, else Playwright's default folder), so no test downloads a browser; the test passes its root to the engine as `GOODFETCH_BROWSERS_PATH` (`BROWSERS_PATH_VARIABLE`). A build Playwright has not installed fails with `BrowserCacheError::NotInstalled`, naming `pnpm exec playwright install chromium`. A test that searches every file of the Goodfetch home, such as the reset gate, takes `browser_cache_in_home(&env)` instead: the same build in the home's own Browser cache (`<home>/browsers`, where an engine without `GOODFETCH_BROWSERS_PATH` keeps it), so the search takes in the cache as a person's home holds it, and sets `egress.offline = true`, so a build the engine cannot find fails at once with `artifact_unavailable_offline` rather than downloading some 150 MB within a call's deadline (fix-queue 124).
  - **A test of `browser clean` lays out a stand-in build with `lay_out_browser_entry(root, build, used_at)`**: its placeholder files, lock and `entry.json`, installed and last used at `used_at`, in the Browser cache at `root`.
  - **A test that needs a Daemon starts the one of its own home**, `Daemon::start(&env, Product::Collie)` (`Product::Foxhound` for a test that needs Foxhound, ADR 0094): the tool manifest's executable of that Product runs `daemon launched --keep-alive` under the test's environment, so it is the test's own child, joins its record and never stops for idleness. It reads back the runtime file the Daemon prints once ready from `<home>/<major>/daemon.json`; `description()` holds its `socket` (the Unix socket's path, or the loopback TCP address on Windows) and `token`. `stop(&env)` runs the executable's `daemon stop` and fails with `DaemonError::StillRunning` or `DaemonError::RuntimeFileLeft` when the process did not end or a runtime file is left in the home; dropping a value the test did not stop runs the same `daemon stop`, so the Daemon ends its Runs and its children before it exits, and kills it only when that fails or outlasts the deadline. A Daemon that exits before it is ready fails with `DaemonError::Exited`, its own message on stderr. `goodfetch-test-env --daemon` (Collie's) or `--daemon=foxhound` starts one, describes it under `daemon`, stops it when its stdin closes and exits non-zero when that stop fails.
  - **A Rust Journey variant runs through `JourneyRun`** ([Journeys](#tests-and-results)): `JourneyRun::start(id, surface, engine, ResultDocument::journey)` reads `journeys/<id>.toml` as plain values, refuses a surface the Journey does not list (`JourneyRunError::UnlistedSurface`; `foxhound-cli` counts only on a Collie Journey that lists `cli`), starts the variant's `TestEnv` and, when the file has a `[proving_ground]` table, its Proving ground on the Journey's own seed, and records the Product and version the executable's `--version` reports. `run.step(wording, |run| …)`, `run.check(wording, |run| …)` and `run.expect_task(check, &submission)` record the outcomes; a body fails when it panics or returns an `Err`. `run.command()` is the executable's command line under the variant's environment, `run.env()`, `run.engine()` and `run.proving_ground()` the rest; `run.surface_package(name, version)` names an `sdk-rust` variant's crate. `run.finish()` hands the facts to the constructor, writes its document and returns a `Finished` whose `assert_proved()` fails the test with a `VariantNotProved` unless the document says `proved` and nothing failed. A run dropped without `finish` fails the test with an `UnfinishedJourneyRun`. `JourneyRun::start_file(path, …)` runs a Journey file outside the catalog, for test support's own tests.
  - **A crash or fault test records an Observation log**, `ObservationLog::for_test(&env)`: plain `Record`s of a host, an optional simulated time and an `Operation` (`Fence`, `Write`, `Commit` and `Visible` for delivery; `Limit`, `Reserve` and `Settle` for Usage; `Fault`; or the test's own `Other`). Values are `ValueDigest`s and security values `Redacted`. When the test fails, the log is written as NDJSON to `observation-log.ndjson` in the kept folder, listed as `goodfetch-observation-log=<path>`. `fencing(&log, class)` judges delivery by Guarantee class and `limits(&log, allowed_loss)` judges Usage; each returns the first `Violation` with the records that show it. `kill_at(&process, &moment, &mut log)` kills a process started through `env.spawn` at a `Moment` (the n-th protocol message whose JSON-RPC `method` is the event), drawn by `Moment::drawn(env.seed(), events, occurrences)`, and records the kill. A moment drawn among requests the test leaves unanswered needs them all in flight at once whatever the machine: the home's governor holds that many HTTP admissions at pressure (Foxhound's `in_flight_whatever_the_machine`), as one sized by a loaded machine's memory admits only its floor of 1.
  - **A test that reads an Attached engine's or the stdio MCP server's stdout reads it through `StrictStdout`**, with the child's logging at `trace` and its stderr piped: `StrictStdout::of(&process)` for a child of `env.spawn`, `StrictStdout::from_output(&output)` for one run by `command(&env, program)`. It yields each newline-delimited JSON-RPC 2.0 message (request, notification or reply) as a plain JSON value, and any other line fails the test, naming the line and showing its bytes escaped with the child's stderr: a log line, an ANSI escape, a byte-order mark, bytes that are not UTF-8, a truncated message, a partial line at the end of stdout, or JSON that is not a JSON-RPC message, a batch included. Iterating panics with a `StrictStdoutError`; `try_next()` returns it; each line waits at most `MESSAGE_DEADLINE` (10 s), or what `within(deadline)` sets. `is_quiet_for(quiet)` says whether the child writes nothing for a short duration the test sets, such as while a call that no credit lets through must stay unanswered; a line written meanwhile is kept for the next read. It proves only an absence, so a test uses it after a state that makes the absence the product's, never to wait for something to happen.
  - **A process fault an SDK or the CLI must handle comes from `goodfetch-scripted-engine`** ([ADR 0069](docs/adr/0069-sdks-and-the-cli-are-tested-against-a-scripted-engine-and-the-binary-of-the-same-commit.md)), playing a script of `test-support/fixtures/engine-scripts/` (`engine_script(name)`), whose format is `Script`: a `description` and `steps`, each one of `expect` (a method and its params, matched on their canonical JSON), `reply` (a `result` or the wire's `error` object, sent with the ID of the request the last `expect` of a request matched; an expected notification between them, such as that request's `$/cancel`, leaves it to answer), `send` (a `request` to a Client's handler or a `notification` such as an event), `await_reply`, `stall`, `close`, `raw` (text that is not JSON-RPC) and `exit` (a code). An SDK reaches it as an Attached engine: its Product's engine-path variable in the environment map names the executable, which the SDK starts as `attached-engine`, and the test copies the script to `ATTACHED_SCRIPT` (`scripted-engine/script.json`) under the map's `GOODFETCH_HOME`; `--script <file> --mode stdio [--record <file>]` names one directly. The CLI and an SDK reach it as a Daemon through `--mode socket`: it writes the Daemon's runtime file, `RuntimeFile` (`endpoint`, `token`, `version`, `pid`), at `RuntimeFile::path(home, version)`, `<home>/<major>/daemon.json` under the map's `GOODFETCH_HOME`, mode `0600`, and listens on a `0600` Unix socket beside it (in its own folder under `/tmp` when that path is too long for the OS, as on macOS, which the test's `TestEnv` removes at teardown when a killed engine left it) or on loopback TCP on Windows (ADR 0024), and holds the Daemon's serving lock, `DAEMON_SERVING_LOCK` (`daemon.lock`) beside the file, until it exits, as a live Daemon does: a Client takes a runtime file whose lock nobody holds for a dead Daemon's. `--token` (default `SCRIPTED_DAEMON_TOKEN`) and `--product-version` (default this commit's) fill the file; the token check is the script's own `expect` of a `hello` carrying it. It prints `{"address", "runtime_file"}` once a Client can find it, which `reported_address` reads, and removes the file and the socket when the play ends, unless the script ends with `exit`, which leaves them as a crashed Daemon does (`daemon/dead-pid.json`). Started as `daemon launched`, as a Product's `daemon start` starts its Daemon, it plays `ATTACHED_SCRIPT` in socket mode and prints the runtime file itself once ready, as a launched Daemon does; the conformance harness starts a Daemon target this way. `--mode tcp` listens on loopback port 0 and prints `{"address"}`. In both, the one Client is accepted when a step first reads or writes. It records every message it read and wrote, one JSON line each, at `ATTACHED_RECORD` beside the script, and on a message no step expects, one that is not a JSON-RPC 2.0 message (no `"jsonrpc": "2.0"`, or both `result` and `error`), or a step left unused it exits 1 with `goodfetch-scripted-engine: <code>: <reason>` on stderr, `<code>` being `unexpected_message`, `unused_step` or `nothing_to_answer`, or `publish_failed` or `listen_failed` when it cannot start listening; a failed play keeps its own code even when removing its runtime file fails too. A test matches the code. A Quick-suite test of `goodfetch-test-support` validates every message step of the corpus against the committed bundle and catalog, and refuses a method that neither the catalog nor the connection has (the Client's `hello`, `$/cancel` and `$/credit`, the engine's `$/events` and `$/chunk`) and an Error code the catalog does not define; `raw` steps are skipped, and a `send` request is checked as a JSON-RPC request only, until the catalog declares the requests an engine sends a Client.
  - **A test of run state crashes SQLite through the crash VFS**, `CrashVfs::register(name, seed)` (name from `env.unique_name`, seed from `env.seed()`): the code under test opens its database with `vfs.name()` as its VFS value. `vfs.crash(CrashKind::Process | CrashKind::PowerCut)` crashes the instance and returns the `Crash`, which says what `Landed` of each file's unsynced writes; `vfs.fail_at(index, IoFault::Error | IoFault::Full)` injects a fault at the operation numbered `index` (`vfs.operations()` is the next one's). `after_crash(&vfs, path)` opens the database again and fails with `AfterCrashError::Damaged` unless `PRAGMA integrity_check` passes; the test checks its acknowledged commits on the connection it returns. Files live in memory, so the database's path is a name, not a file in the test's folder. `unique_name` counts per `TestEnv`, so a model-based test that registers a VFS in each case adds the case's own folder name to it.
  - **A test that power-cuts a real executable mounts its folders through LazyFS** (Linux), `LazyFs::mount(dir)`, in a setup made with `TestEnv::for_lazyfs()`: its folder is in `/tmp`, or in the folder `GOODFETCH_LAZYFS_FOLDER` names, whatever `TMPDIR` says, because the operating system refuses a user's FUSE mount outside some folders (Ubuntu's AppArmor profile of `fusermount3` allows `$HOME`, `/mnt`, `/run/user/<uid>`, `/media` and `/tmp`), and such a refusal fails with `LazyFsError::MountRefused`, which says so. The folder keeps its path, its files seen through the pinned LazyFS build that `GOODFETCH_LAZYFS` names, whose cache holds every write until it is synced. After the kill, `clear_cache()` drops what was never synced and returns once LazyFS says so; dropping the value ends the mount, syncing every file first as a clean shutdown would, and a second `mount` of the folder is the remount after the reboot. A mount that cannot be made, or a LazyFS that is not set, fails with a `LazyFsError` carrying LazyFS's output, never a skip. `just lazyfs-test` fetches LazyFS's pinned source archives (`test-support/lazyfs/sources.toml`, each checked against its SHA-256 by `xtask lazyfs fetch` before the deny), builds them (`xtask lazyfs build`, needing cmake, a C++17 compiler and FUSE 3) and runs the `lazyfs::` tests, which nextest's profiles otherwise leave out.
  - **A test that fills a size-capped filesystem runs its executable over a tmpfs** (Linux; the Storage conformance cases and the Daemon log cases, ADR 0122): `tmpfs_mounted(&env, folder, size, program)`, or `tmpfs_mounted_home(&env, size, program)` over the Goodfetch home with its `logs` folder kept on the test's own disk, is a command that mounts it in a user and mount namespace of the started process (`ci/os-services.sh setup`'s `unshare`), failing at once, naming the restriction, when that `unshare` cannot map the test's user to root. The test reaches the filesystem through `inside_mount(pid, path)`, fills it to a given room with `fill_filesystem(pid, folder, room)` and frees it with `free_filesystem(&filler)`.
  - **A test that searches what `tracing` wrote for a value no log line may hold runs its work under `logged(work)`**: every level, without colour, beside the work's answer.
  - **A SQLite format's corpus writer dumps its store with `sql_dump(&connection)`**: every table's schema and rows, then its indexes, as SQL text that `xtask compat` reads, byte-stable (tables and indexes in name order, rows in `rowid` order).
  - **A layer's SSRF runner applies the one table** ([Egress and hostile input](#egress-and-hostile-input)): `SsrfTable::committed()` reads `test-support/fixtures/ssrf/cases.toml`, whose JSON Schema `xtask codegen` commits, and `run_cases(cases, public, layer)` calls the layer once per `SsrfCase` (its URL, route, scripted lookups, redirect, fetches, domain policy and target, a target written `public` being the layer's public stand-in `public`), compares the `Outcome` (the typed refusal, and the addresses connections reached, from the Proving ground's connection record end to end) with the case's expected refusal, and fails with every case the layer let through. `ScriptedResolver::new(case.script(public))` answers a case's lookups in order, `public` being the layer's public stand-in; a lookup beyond the script or of another name panics with a `LookupFailure`, again when the resolver drops if the code under test swallowed it. A Quick-suite test fails when a block of the committed IANA registries that is not globally reachable has no case, and parsing fails on a case of an unknown family. Collie's runners take their cases through `Runner::committed(layer, file!(), "<test function>")`, which gives each the cases of the families its entry in `test-support/fixtures/ssrf/layers.toml` names, and run them with `Runner::run(controls, public, layer)`, which also runs its positive controls (each must be let through and reach its target) and fails when it has none.
  - **A layer's hostile-input runner applies the family tables** ([Egress and hostile input](#egress-and-hostile-input)): `HostileTables::committed()` reads `test-support/fixtures/hostile/` (`header_values`, `secret_redirects`, `paths`, `schema_refs` and `limits`), whose JSON Schemas `xtask codegen` commits. Each `HostileCase` gives its `input`, the `expected` typed error, matched on its Error code, and `served_by`: the Proving ground task whose page serves it end to end, a page `waiting_on_owner` or a `missing_page`, or `program_input`. A case with no expected error fails the parse, and a Pre-merge test fails on a page the Proving ground has no task for. A path case runs on the OSes its `os` names, `cases_here()` on the running one. Pair the cases with the layer's own positive control.
  - **A test that proves a child starts no program runs it under `NoExec`** (Linux, x86-64 and 64-bit Arm): `NoExec::new(path).arg(…)`, then `output(&env)` or `spawn(&env)`, starts the executable in the test's folder with its cleared environment under a seccomp filter that refuses `execve` and `execveat` with `EPERM` once the program runs.
  - **A test of a host that refuses Chromium's sandbox its user namespace sets it on the engine's command** (Linux, x86-64 and 64-bit Arm; [Confinement](#confinement)): `under_default_seccomp(&mut command)` runs the child under the namespace rules of Docker's and containerd's default seccomp profiles (add `KUBERNETES_SERVICE_HOST` for a pod's `RuntimeDefault`), `under_apparmor_restriction(&mut command)` under the `unprivileged_userns` profile Ubuntu's restriction moves an unconfined program to, as a browser no AppArmor profile gives `userns` runs; the same command without them is the host with its fix applied, the positive control. `apparmor_restricts_user_namespaces()` says whether the host restricts them, which the AppArmor case asserts rather than skips.
  - **An end-to-end rebinding test resolves through `DnsNamespace`** (Linux; [Egress and hostile input](#egress-and-hostile-input)): `DnsNamespace::enter(&env, dns)`, `dns` being the Proving ground's `fixtures.dns`, then `output(&env, program, args)` runs the executable in a network and mount namespace of its own, with the test's environment, whose `/etc/resolv.conf` names `127.0.0.1`, relayed to the fixture, and whose `nsswitch.conf` resolves `hosts` through `files dns`; `lo` also carries `127.0.0.2`, so a resolver that asks only for the families the host has (`AI_ADDRCONFIG`) asks for IPv4. `goodfetch-test-env dns-namespace` makes it, as the test's user in an unprivileged user namespace, or through `sudo -n` where the kernel refuses one (a hosted Ubuntu runner), then starting the program as the test's user. A runner declares its way in `GOODFETCH_TEST_DNS_NAMESPACE` (`user_namespace` or `sudo`): the hosted Linux cells of the Release suite declare `sudo`, and the owner's machine's VM, whose job container has `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, `user_namespace`. A namespace that cannot be made is `DnsNamespaceError::Unavailable`, naming each way's refusal, which fails the test.
  - **A test plants a Secret canary of each kind it hands to the code under test, and searches every output for it** ([Secrets](#secrets), ADR 0067): `Canaries::new(env.seed())`, then `plant(kind)` for a typed password, a TOTP seed, a one-time code, a proxy password, an HTTP authentication password, a header or JSON value, a `caller:` answer, a model API key, a webhook signing secret, a Sink credential, a Session cookie, an endpoint token or the run data key (a fresh value each time, drawn from the seed; one that may hold any character ends with `"\+/=&`, so JSON escaping and URL encoding change it), which the test passes through a Secret provider's real interface, recording where that keeps it with `record_planted(id, output)`. `plant_short(kind)` plants a short canary, seven characters under `SHORT_VALUE` (the short-value rule), which `gitleaks` cannot know. `plant_through(&proving_ground, kind)` plants the four kinds the Proving ground's `canaries` login flows take, with the `account()` that logs in, and `sightings(&proving_ground)` lists each request that carried one, `Planted` where its flow sends it and `Leaked` elsewhere. `search(outputs)` (files, folders, named bytes, or a page read, `Output::Read`, searched for every canary but a short one) returns every `Hit` raw, JSON-escaped, URL-encoded, in base64 (standard or URL-safe, at any alignment, so inside a Basic header) and in hex, with its output, byte offset and `Placement`; `leaks(outputs)` keeps the leaks. A canary's `Debug` form and every hit name it by ID, never by value. TypeScript's `searchCanaries(canaries, outputs)` and Python's `search_canaries(canaries, outputs)` search the SDKs' `toString`, `repr` and errors the same way, and all three pass `test-support/fixtures/canaries/search.json`. `gitleaks_rule()` is the `gitleaks` rule, one per kind, which `xtask codegen` commits to `test-support/fixtures/canaries/gitleaks.toml` for CI's output scan, and `ci/canary-rule.sh` (`just lint`) shows it failing on each fixture artifact that holds a canary and passing the clean one. Collie's `tests/it/canaries.rs` holds the registry of where each kind is planted through Collie, or the ticket that plants it; a kind it calls planted that its named test does not plant fails.
  - **Zone, locale and text cases come from one table and one record**, committed once in `test-support/fixtures/` and read by all three languages ([ADR 0073](docs/adr/0073-accessibility-meets-wcag-2-2-aa-the-console-ships-ready-to-translate-and-screenshots-compare-in-one-pinned-image.md)): `locales()` (`en-US`, `de-DE`, `fr-FR`, `tr-TR`, `ar-EG`, `hi-IN`, `ja-JP`) and `time_zones()` (`UTC`, `America/St_Johns`, `Asia/Kathmandu`, `Pacific/Chatham`, `Australia/Lord_Howe`, and `Europe/Paris` and `America/New_York` with their 2026 days of DST gap and overlap) from `locales.json`, and `unicode_record()` from `unicode-record.json`: one Record's fields of hard text (bidi text with marks, one word in NFC and NFD, a ZWJ emoji sequence, an astral character, a zero-width joiner, a leading byte-order mark, a tab and a newline), which a Sink or Export test round-trips byte for byte. TypeScript names them `locales()`, `timeZones()` and `unicodeRecord()`.
- **`goodfetch-test-support` depends on no Goodfetch crate**, and product crates list it only under `[dev-dependencies]`. CI checks both.
  _Why:_ a helper crate that depended on the crate under test would give that crate's tests two copies of its types; values are all ADR 0059 injects.
- **A test adapter that implements a Goodfetch interface is its own test-only crate** ([ADR 0125](docs/adr/0125-a-test-adapter-of-a-goodfetch-interface-is-its-own-test-only-crate-which-no-unit-test-of-a-crate-it-depends-on-takes.md)): `goodfetch-simulation` (turmoil's network adapter, ADR 0087), `goodfetch-scripted-model` (the Scripted model, ADR 0065), `goodfetch-recording-gate` (the Recording gate, which the page crate's integration tests put on every page in place of Foxhound's navigation gate, lease check and response observer, #720) and `goodfetch-stub-gateway` (the Stub gateway, a stand-in of Goodfetch Cloud's gateway that no crate depends on, an executable its own tests start, ADR 0096), and any later one on CI's list, which the pull request that adds it extends. Each is `publish = false`, taken only under `[dev-dependencies]`, banned from each Product's executable, and never taken by a unit test of a crate it depends on, directly or through other crates; that crate's integration tests (`tests/it`) may take it. CI checks all four. When code that calls the interface is unit-tested on the adapter, the interface sits in a crate apart from that code, as the Model provider interface sits apart from JSON repair, the bounded retry and Usage.
  _Why:_ a unit test of a crate the adapter depends on would link two copies of that crate, whose types do not match; any other crate links one, so the Scripted model's unit tests of AI logic stay in the Quick suite.
- **The test versions of `Clock` and `Entropy` are `Clock::paused(start)` and `Entropy::seeded(seed)`**, in the crate that defines the handles. `disallowed-methods` refuses both; each test module, integration-test root or bench target's root ([ADR 0071](docs/adr/0071-performance-budgets-are-counted-per-change-and-time-is-measured-on-the-reference-host.md)) that calls them opts in with `#[expect(clippy::disallowed_methods, reason = "…")]` (`#![expect]` in `tests/it/main.rs`), never with a crate-wide or `cfg_attr(test, …)` attribute in product code. `main` builds only the system versions. Tests that pause Tokio's clock enable its `test-util` feature under `[dev-dependencies]`. The seed and the start time come from `goodfetch-test-support`.
- **TypeScript and Python reuse the Rust setup, never rewrite it.** The `goodfetch-test-env` executable of `goodfetch-test-support` prepares a test's folder, home and environment, starts what it is asked to, prints one JSON description (paths, environment map, seed, addresses), and tears everything down when its standard input closes.
  - `@goodfetch/test-support` (`test-support/typescript/`, private) gives Vitest fixtures through `test.extend`: a test imports `test` from it and takes `testEnv` (the description, validated against `test-support/fixtures/schemas/`) or `env` (the map an SDK takes: that environment with the engine-path variable of the test's Product, Collie's unless `test.override({ product: "foxhound" })`). Every process started through its `processes` record, `goodfetch-test-env` included, must have ended at teardown, or the test fails. A test that sets `test.override("provingGroundSeed", <seed>)` takes `provingGround`, the Proving ground `goodfetch-test-env --proving-ground <seed>` started, with the same fields as Rust's `description()`; without a seed the fixture fails with `proving_ground_unseeded`. A test that sets `test.override("withDaemon", true)` takes `daemon`, the `socket` and `token` of the Daemon `goodfetch-test-env --daemon=<product>` started in its home, run by its `product`'s executable; without it the fixture fails with `daemon_not_requested`.
  - **An SDK unit test plays a script in process** with `playScript(name)`, `name` being a script of the corpus such as `"self-test/call-answered.json"`, checked against `script.schema.json` (`fixture_invalid` otherwise). It returns `transport`, the in-memory transport the SDK takes (a `ReadableWritablePair<Uint8Array, Uint8Array>`: the engine's bytes to read and the Client's to write, newline-delimited JSON-RPC each way as on an Attached engine's stdio; closing `writable` closes the Client's end), `messages`, every message read and written as `goodfetch-scripted-engine` records them, and `done()`. It plays each step as the executable does; a message no step expects rejects the Client's write and its next read at once with `unexpected_message`, and `done()`, which ends the play as the Client closing its end would, throws `unused_step` for a step left unused, or returns how the script ended (`finished`, `closed`, or `exit` with its code).
  - `journeyDocument(facts)` and `writeJourneyDocument(path, facts)` build a Journey variant's `journey` result document on the types `xtask codegen` generates from the bundle (`src/generated/`); the facts hold no outcome, and the document is frozen with the outcome derived ([Journeys](#tests-and-results)). `conformanceDocument(facts)` and `writeConformanceDocument(path, facts)` do the same for a `conformance` document, by `goodfetch-protocol`'s rules, from a header and each declared capability's cases with their entries. `journeyVectors(variant)` and `conformanceVectors(header)` give the shared outcome vectors as facts, and `parseCaseList`/`readCaseList` read a suite's case list (`case_list_invalid` otherwise).
  - `@goodfetch/test-support/case-log`'s `CaseLogReporter({ out, caseList })` writes the case log of Client conformance cases ([SDKs](#sdks)), each entry checked against the bundle's `case_log_entry` schema (`case_log_entry_invalid` otherwise); `vitest.config.ts` runs it into `target/case-logs/vitest.jsonl` and declares `clientCaseTags(readClientCases(caseList))` as tags. A test records its checks and evidence through the `clientCase` fixture (`check(name, assertion)`, `message`, `oracleAnswer`, `observation`), refused with `client_case_untagged` on a test with no `client_case/` tag.
  - Its Playwright fixtures are `@goodfetch/test-support/playwright`'s `test`: a served test imports `test` and `expect` from it, never from `@playwright/test`. A test whose Console keeps its address across a Daemon restart sets the `namedConsolePort` option, a port outside the OS's ephemeral range that test support claims for it (`reserveNamedPort`): never restart a Daemon on a port the OS chose, which another program can take in between (#2035). Its `context` and `request` are Playwright's own, refused with `bypass_csp_refused` when the test sets `bypassCSP` or `contextOptions.bypassCSP`; `failOnErrors`, on in every test, records each page's errors (`watchPageErrors`) and fails the test with `page_errors` at teardown, except those a test that provokes them on purpose, such as the production CSP's refusals, asserts and forgets with `failOnErrors.take()`; `coversKnownFeatures`, on in every test, fails it with `covers_unknown` at teardown when a `covers` annotation names a Feature the configuration's `metadata.features` does not list, or the configuration passes no such list. `axe`, on in every test, audits each open page once the test has passed (`auditAccessibility`, before `failOnErrors` judges the pages) and fails it with `axe_violations`, `axe_exception_unmatched` or `axe_exception_too_broad`; `expectAccessible(page)` runs the same audit on the way ([Accessibility](#accessibility)). A served Console test takes `daemon`: a Daemon of its own, started by `goodfetch-test-env --daemon=<product>` in a home of its own and run by the executable of its `product` option (`test.use({ product: "foxhound" })` for a Foxhound screen), with the Console the `consoleServer` option serves on port 0 (`url`, `port`), by default `executableConsole`, the Console the Daemon serves itself from its executable's Console build at the URL its runtime file reports, and `provingGround` when it sets `provingGroundSeed`; at teardown the test's pages are audited and closed, so no Console reaches for a Daemon the harness stopped, then it stops, leaving no runtime file and no process. Its `executable` is the one running the Daemon and `env` the test's cleared environment; the `engine` option runs the Daemon on another executable, for a skew run. The `sizeCapped` option, a size in bytes, puts the test's whole folder on a size-capped filesystem of its own ([ADR 0122](docs/adr/0122-a-run-stops-resumably-before-its-disk-fills-and-a-daemons-logs-rotate-at-a-bounded-size.md)): a tmpfs mounted in a user and mount namespace that `goodfetch-test-env` starts in, through the `unshare` `ci/os-services.sh setup` installs, on Linux only (`size_capped_unsupported` elsewhere, where a test of it skips until test support makes the small disk image). The test reaches it through `daemon.sizeCapped` (`outside(path)` under `/proc/<pid>/root`, `fill(folder, room)` and `free()`), and `daemon.run(args)` runs the executable in the test's home, inside those namespaces through `nsenter` when there are any. `executableConsole`'s sign-in link is the one the executable's `console --json --no-browser` prints, which carries the Daemon's token in its fragment; it has no single sign-on yet, so a test of one passes the server that stands in for it, and it fails with `console_not_served` when the Daemon serves no Console. `signIn` installs the virtual authenticator on the context (`credentials.install()`) and signs a person in the way they do: `withLink(page)` follows the executable's single-user sign-in link and waits until the page has taken its token out of the address, `withOidc(page)` signs `OIDC_PERSON` in through the worker's local OIDC provider (`oidc-provider`, at the version the package pins, its tarball pinned by the lockfile's SHA-512 digest), each failing with `sign_in_failed` unless the Console then answers its session address as signed in. Neither writes a cookie: `context` and `request` refuse a `storageState` with `storage_state_refused`, and oxlint refuses `addCookies`, `setStorageState` and `document.cookie` in test files.
  - `@goodfetch/test-support/protocol-adapter`'s `createProtocolAdapter({ product })`, called inside a test, is the in-memory adapter of the protocol a Console component or screen test passes to the Console's Client ([Components](#components)), in Node or Vitest Browser Mode. `transport.open(listener)` opens a Connection carrying JSON-RPC text; the adapter answers `hello` itself, refusing as the engine does (the generated refusal vectors of `crates/goodfetch-protocol/generated/refusals/protocol/`) a Product it does not carry with `not_in_product` and another protocol version with `protocol_version_unsupported`, and `engine.info`, unless the test set an answer, with the engine `hello` names, since the Console Client's stall watchdog asks it of a Connection silent for a stall timeout; `answer(operation, valueOrFunction)` answers every call of an operation, in any order, with a result or `failure(code, details)`, typed by the generated catalog types, and `requests` lists what the Client sent. `push(event, position)` streams an event to each subscription as its credits allow; `drop()` closes every Connection, and a subscription that resumes `after` a position continues from it, or, after `forgetPositions()`, starts with `events.resync`; `restart()` stands for a restarted Daemon, a new engine instance whose positions count from 1 again; `$/cancel` answers `call_cancelled`. `serve(handle, bytes)` gives `bodyFetcher(handle)` a body's bytes. Every message and answer is checked with Ajv against the committed bundle and catalog of `crates/goodfetch-protocol/generated/`: a value `answer` or `push` refuses throws `answer_invalid` or `event_invalid` at once, and a call the test did not answer, a request or a function's answer the bundle refuses, and a body never served fail the test at its end with `call_unanswered`, `request_invalid`, `answer_invalid` or `body_unserved`, even when the Client swallowed them. Once its test ends, it closes every Connection without telling the Client, so a late answer or event reaches no later test's Client; a function answer still pending then is dropped unchecked, since no Client saw it. Its `transport` and `bodyFetcher` are the shapes the Console's Client takes (`console/collie/src/client/client.ts`, `createConsoleClient`), beside a `PageVisibility` the test sets.
  - `@goodfetch/test-support/features`'s `readFeatureIds(root)` gives the Feature IDs of `features/inventory.json`, checked against its committed schema (`inventory_invalid` otherwise), which `vitest.config.ts` declares as tags and `playwright.config.ts` passes as `metadata.features`.
  - `goodfetch-test-support` (`test-support/python/`, never published) is a pytest plugin registered through `pytest11`.
    - `test_env` is the test's `TestEnv`: `folder`, `home`, the cleared `env` map, `seed`, `start_time` and `tools`, from the description `goodfetch-test-env --test-name <node ID>` prints, checked against `test-support/fixtures/schemas/`. `test_env.start(args)` starts a process in the folder with the cleared map, and the teardown, which closes `goodfetch-test-env`'s stdin, fails the test when one is still alive.
    - `env` is the map an SDK takes as `env=`: the cleared map with the engine-path variable of the test's `product`, `GOODFETCH_COLLIE_ENGINE_PATH` or `GOODFETCH_FOXHOUND_ENGINE_PATH`, naming that Product's executable from the test tool manifest. `product` is `Product.COLLIE`; a test that needs Foxhound overrides the fixture with `Product.FOXHOUND`.
    - `write_journey_document(path, *, …)` writes a Journey variant's `journey` result document from the pydantic models `xtask codegen` generates from the bundle (`goodfetch_test_support.generated.result_document`), takes no outcome, and returns the one it derived ([Journeys](#tests-and-results)).
    - `proving_ground` is the Proving ground `goodfetch-test-env --proving-ground <seed>` started, a `ProvingGround` with the same fields as Rust's `description()`. A test sets its seed by overriding the `proving_ground_seed` fixture, which is `None` by default; without one the fixture fails with `ProvingGroundUnseeded`.
    - `daemon` is the Daemon `goodfetch-test-env --daemon=<product>` started in the test's home, a `Daemon` with its `socket` and `token`, run by the executable of the test's `product`. A test asks for it by overriding the `with_daemon` fixture to return `True` (`False` by default); without it the fixture fails with `DaemonNotRequested`.
    - It registers the `journey` and `covers` markers, and `pytest --collect-only --marker-listing=<file>` writes every collected test's node ID with each `journey` marker's keywords and the `covers` values as JSON, for the Feature scanner and the Journey check.
- **The `test-util` feature of `goodfetch-collie` and `goodfetch-foxhound` is for people who embed them**, never Goodfetch's own helper home.
- **Expected results of Proving ground pages come from its oracle**, never copied into test code.
- **A helper written twice moves into test support.**

## Fixtures and data files

- **Fixtures live in a `fixtures/` folder beside the tests that read them.** Data several languages read lives once, in `test-support/fixtures/`.
- **A test copies a fixture into its temporary folder before changing it.**
- **A fixture is data, never code the repository runs or lints:** Vitest, Playwright, pytest, ruff, pyright and mypy skip every `fixtures/` folder, so a fixture workspace may hold tests and settings that break the rules on purpose.
- **No fixture file exceeds 256 KiB.** Larger or repetitive data is generated from a seed, by the Proving ground or a generator in test support. No Git LFS.
- **A generated fixture or a recorded exchange has a `<name>.source.toml` file beside it,** naming the generator or recording job, its version, the seed or recording date, and the command that regenerates it. Secrets are redacted from recordings.
  _Why:_ a fixture nobody can regenerate becomes untouchable.

## Snapshots

- **Tools:** insta in Rust, Vitest's snapshots in TypeScript, inline-snapshot in Python.
- **Inline for short values** (about 20 lines); a file for longer ones: insta's `.snap` files, Vitest's `toMatchFileSnapshot` or `toMatchSnapshot`, inline-snapshot's `external()`.
- **What snapshots cover:** structured outputs (JSON-RPC responses, CLI output, generated surfaces, extraction output). Never instead of a precise assertion on one value.
- **Every varying value is redacted or filtered** (IDs, times, durations, temporary paths), with the shared settings of test support.
  - The rules are committed once, `test-support/fixtures/snapshot-filters.json`: the test's folder, the executable's Product version and commit, matched literally, then ULIDs and Schedule IDs, RFC 3339 instants, durations and port numbers on loopback hosts. A value no rule names stays, so a change to it fails the snapshot.
  - Rust binds `snapshot_settings(&SnapshotValues::of(&env).with_executable(version, commit))` around its insta assertions; TypeScript and Python snapshot `redact(output, values)` (`{ folder, productVersion, commit }`; `folder=`, `product_version=`, `commit=`).
  - A path the output prints that no rule names, such as an executable's in a `fix`, is named with `.with_path(path, "[COLLIE]")`, never a filter of its own: it is matched in the folder's forms, its canonical path among them, since an executable names itself by the path the OS gives it, canonical on Linux, and a build folder reached through a link (`CARGO_TARGET_DIR` on another disk) prints in another form than the test's path. Rust's `redact(text, &values)` applies the same rules to a value a test compares without a snapshot.
- **CI never writes a snapshot and fails on a mismatched, missing or orphaned one:** `cargo insta test --check --unreferenced reject`; Vitest in CI; inline-snapshot, which raises on an empty `snapshot()` there. While the quarantine list skips a nextest test, its snapshots look orphaned, so `just test` lets unreferenced insta snapshots warn instead, and says so, until the test leaves the list.
- **Snapshots change only through the tool's update or review command, never by hand:** `just snapshots [packages]` runs the tests and reviews insta's changes (`cargo insta test --review`), updates the changed pnpm packages' Vitest snapshots (`-u`), and reviews inline-snapshot's (`--inline-snapshot=review`); `just snapshots accept` writes them all without review. inline-snapshot updates run single-process (`-n0`), since under xdist it can neither create nor fix one. CI runs neither.
- **A snapshot change is reviewed as a change of expected behaviour.**

## Property tests, fuzzing and doctests

- **Parsers and stateful rules get property tests:** `proptest` in Rust, `hypothesis` in Python. Their regression files are committed.
- **Untrusted input** gets a `cargo-fuzz` target for every decoder of input Goodfetch did not write itself, replayed by the `#[test]` that `fuzz_replay!` writes, on every OS. The list and the rest of fuzzing are under [Security](#security) (ADR 0067).
- **Doctests only on the public crates** (`goodfetch-collie`, `goodfetch-foxhound` and `goodfetch-collie-client`, ADRs 0090 and 0095), where they are the examples of ADR 0030. `goodfetch-collie-client`'s doctests connect through `goodfetch-collie`, a path-only dev-dependency, which `cargo publish` drops, since a published crate keeps only the dev-dependencies that specify a `version` ([Cargo reference](https://doc.rust-lang.org/cargo/reference/specifying-dependencies.html#development-dependencies)).

## Injection and determinism

Decided in [ADR 0059](docs/adr/0059-dependencies-are-injected-as-values-and-only-time-and-randomness-as-handles.md).

### What is injected, and how

| Dependency | Injected as | In tests |
|---|---|---|
| Wall time, monotonic time, sleeps | the `Clock` handle | its test version under Tokio's paused clock |
| Random choices (jitter, pool picks, fingerprint seeds, ID randomness) | the `Entropy` handle | a fixed seed, printed at test start |
| Secret randomness (keys, tokens, run data keys, CAs) | nothing: the OS generator | the OS generator |
| Data, caches and configuration | the `GOODFETCH_HOME` value | a per-test temporary folder |
| SQLite and files | paths under that home | real files |
| Listen addresses | a value (port 0, or a socket path) | the bound address reported back |
| Host names | the `resolve` value | Proving ground hosts mapped to loopback |
| Environment | an explicit map | a cleared map for each child process |
| Keychain, service manager, processes | nothing: the real service | the real service, unique names |

- **A dependency with a local stand-in gets no handle.** Only `Clock` and `Entropy` are swapped in tests, and, in the [Simulation](#simulation) alone, `transport`'s network (ADR 0060). That network is a value with product adapters, the OS and the in-memory pair the Products' embeddable crates use, so its interface is public for them, not for tests, and it is not a third handle.
  _Why:_ a fake passes where the real thing fails, and a stand-in tests what users run.
- **Nothing reads time, chooses at random or reads the environment through a global.** `disallowed-methods` refuses `SystemTime::now`, std and Tokio `Instant::now` and constructors such as `Ulid::new` outside `Clock` and `Entropy`, and `std::env::var` outside `main`; `std::env::set_var` is `unsafe`.

### Time

- **A rule that depends on time is a pure function that takes "now"**, tested with no clock: lease expiry, a Schedule's next occurrence (DST included), backoff. The Politeness store's granted shares stay in its Lua script and are tested against a real Valkey.
- **The backoff's jitter, `Retry-After` and the resolver's cache are such functions** ([ADR 0118](docs/adr/0118-every-fetch-tier-follows-redirects-as-the-browser-does-fails-a-cut-body-and-resends-only-what-a-site-never-processed.md)). With a seeded `Entropy`, each backoff wait lies between `politeness.backoff.initial` and three times the previous wait (the first between `initial` and three times it), never below `initial` or above `politeness.backoff.max`, and two domains backing off at once draw different waits. A `Retry-After` in seconds, in each HTTP-date format, against a `Date` header or the engine's clock, `0`, a past date and an unparsable value each give their wait, and one past `politeness.retry_after.max` holds the domain until the site's time and ends the requests it holds at once. A cached answer expires at its TTL, a negative one at its negative TTL, a server failure is never kept, and an answer whose every address failed to connect is dropped.
- **Code that waits is tested in-process with the test `Clock`** (`#[tokio::test(start_paused = true)]`). Advancing the paused clock moves wall time with it, and `jump_wall` moves wall time alone.
  _Why:_ a default-length lease or a day-long interval then takes no real time.
- **Keep real I/O out of paused-clock tests**, apart from SQLite on tracked blocking threads.
  _Why:_ auto-advance fires whenever the runtime is idle, including during a real socket wait, so a lease can expire early (tokio #4522, #8232).
  - A test that must wait on a real socket under the paused clock, such as the resolver cache's against the Proving ground's DNS fixture, holds the clock still meanwhile: the current-thread runtime never auto-advances while a blocking task runs (`goodfetch-engine`'s `tests/it/held_clock.rs`).
  - The Happy Eyeballs race's end-to-end test relies on auto-advance instead: while its attempt to an address that drops packets waits, the race's attempt delay is the only timer, so the clock moves exactly to it.
- **A test of the binary sets short durations through settings.** No build has a test-only clock control.
  - A short timeout bounds only the call under test: give that call its own `timeout` where it takes one, and keep the Run's `timeout.navigation` at its default. That setting also bounds each command of the browser's launch and of each page's opening, so a Run with a few seconds of it fails its first `run.new_page` on a loaded host (fix-queue 121).
- **Never sleep for real** beyond a short duration the test set itself.
- **A timing-sensitive test runs in nextest's `timing-sensitive` test group** (`.config/nextest.toml`): at most four of them run at once, whatever the run's thread count, and the rest of the suite takes the other threads.
  - A test is timing-sensitive when a check waits on real time against a figure the test or its suite sets: a lease, a navigation timeout, a domain delay or rate, an idle timer, a race's delay, a kill's deadline, or the protocol client's per-message deadline over a long browser run.
  - To tell, look for such a figure in the test or its helpers. Or run it with `NEXTEST_TEST_THREADS=num-cpus` beside other load (`just repeat`, at `nice -n 10`): a timing-sensitive test passes alone and fails, or nears its figure, there.
  - The group selects whole modules of a test binary, so a new test in a listed module joins it. A timing-sensitive test elsewhere goes into a listed module, or its module joins the filter in the change that adds it.
  - The group fixes no flaky test. It keeps the run's own load off the timers and lengthens none of them, and a test in it still asserts no elapsed time.

  _Why:_ nextest starts a binary's tests together, so at every thread the long browser cases of one module ran at once. On a 16-thread host shared with other builds and test runs, their timers then starved with no change to the product. In one of two such runs of every Rust test, three `request_kinds` cases missed their 60 s message deadline. With the group, no timer of its tests failed in two runs at every thread or in five repeats of its tests.
- **A test that takes many cases through browser pages holds one page at a time:** it finishes each Run once that Run's cases are done and closes each case's page before the next opens. The SSRF table's runners on Chromium also run in nextest's `browser-fleet` test group, at most two at once, and a new one joins its filter in the change that adds it.
  _Why:_ while the host's memory is under pressure, the Resource governor admits no page past its floor, one ([ADR 0040](docs/adr/0040-one-resource-governor-per-engine-sized-from-measured-load.md)), and a page past it waits with no answer until the pressure ends. The runners once held about 20 Runs, browsers and pages each until they ended, so on a loaded host their next `run.new_page` outlasted the 60 s message deadline (fix-queue items 107 and 110).
- **A test whose own work keeps several cores busy for minutes runs alone:** the Daemon soak ([Memory at scale](#memory-at-scale)) takes every test thread of the run (`threads-required = "num-test-threads"` in `.config/nextest.toml`), so nextest starts nothing beside it, and its five-minute kill stays the profile's. Such a test fits the kill alone on a loaded host, or it is split or made cheaper; no cap is raised for it.
  _Why:_ its 40 crawls keep the Daemon's two to three cores busy throughout. They took 165 to 210 s alone on the M1 server and 354 to 578 s beside the rest of a Pre-merge run, which timed it out in every M1 deep-fix group's run (#1740): its time followed the run's load, not the code. Since #1765 a Run's pages share the cosmetic selectors it parsed and its crawl settles the attempts that ended together in one pass, so it takes 100 to 142 s alone at load 15 to 28 and 138 to 148 s in a Pre-merge run.
- **Clocks Goodfetch doesn't own stay real:** the Valkey server's (short durations), Chromium's (Proving ground pages take their time step from the task), and the Script sandbox's live `Date.now`, whose tests compare the order of Effects.

### Randomness

- **Every random choice draws from `Entropy`** (`chacha20`, portable across versions).
- **A test runs on a fixed seed.** `GOODFETCH_TEST_SEED` overrides it, and the seed is printed when the test starts.
  _Why:_ a test nextest kills on a timeout never reaches a failure message.
- **Secrets never come from `Entropy`.** The Proving ground's seeded test certificate authority is the one exception.
- **Hash order never reaches output or ordering.** `iter_over_hash_type` is denied; use `BTreeMap` or `IndexMap` where order shows.

### Storage, network and OS services

- **Each test gets its own `GOODFETCH_HOME`** in a temporary folder, and writes nowhere else.
- **Proving ground host names resolve through the `resolve` setting** to loopback, which the run allowlists for tests.
- **Proxies are tested against the Proving ground's real forward proxy.**
- **Local addresses use real extra loopback addresses:** `127.0.0.0/8` on Linux and Windows (Windows not yet tested), `lo0` aliases on macOS, a network namespace under sudo where needed. Tests use `127.0.0.2` to `127.0.0.9`, the count written in `ci/os-services.sh`; macOS answers only on `127.0.0.1` without aliases (checked on macOS 27).
- **No test reaches the public internet.** Only the jobs ADR 0043 names go online, to their named endpoints; every other job runs under its deny with its Egress canary, reaching only its fixtures. Every pinned download, the test V8 build and old releases included, comes before the deny, which allows none of the pinned artifact mirror's hosts (ADR 0043, by the owner's decision of 2026-10-19).
- **Nothing leaves the machine unless configured** (ADR 0043). `xtask telemetry`, in `just lint`, fails on a setting key that names telemetry and on an analytics client in `Cargo.lock`, `uv.lock` or `pnpm-lock.yaml`. A Quick test holds the modules `modules.toml` lets call `goodfetch-engine`'s `http_client` to `pinned_download` and the kinds of Operator-configured endpoint. The Proving ground records each pinned download's request (a plain GET, the user agent `goodfetch-download`), and its connection record shows that `egress.offline` refuses one before it connects.
- **The deny takes the form its runner allows** (`ci/egress/egress.sh`), and every test step after it runs through `ci/vm/exec.sh` or `ci/egress/under-deny.sh`, which `xtask egress check` enforces:
  - in the owner's machine's VM, and on hosted Linux runners in a network namespace, nftables refuse all but the job's allowed endpoints, and the kernel log names every other refused connection;
  - on hosted Windows runners, firewall rules block the product's programs (the workspace's executables and the test runtimes) from all but loopback and the allowed endpoints, and the firewall's log names every other drop. A program the tests copy outside those folders after the deny is not blocked;
  - on the owner's machine without a VM (the Release suite's macOS cells), a sandbox profile refuses every connection but `127.0.0.1` and `::1`, since the runner's standard user cannot change the firewall. The allowed endpoints and `lo0` aliases are refused too, so every download comes before the deny, and a test that connects to an extra loopback address cannot run there yet; one that only binds it can. macOS records no refusal that user can read: the canary proves the sandbox by its `EPERM`, and another refused connection fails only the step that made it.
- **The keychain provider runs against the real keychain** of each OS (`gnome-keyring` started and unlocked on Ubuntu), with a unique service name per test. Consumers of Secrets use the environment or caller provider.
- **Every CI job with a test step runs `ci/os-services.sh setup` before the deny and its first test step**, which `xtask os-services workflows` enforces in `just lint`:
  - on Linux it starts a D-Bus session bus at a path in the job's temporary folder and `gnome-keyring-daemon`'s Secret Service, unlocked with a throwaway password drawn in the job, its keyring files and control socket in that folder too, apart from any keyring of the user's login session; later steps find the bus through `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS`, and `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` stays the user's own runtime folder where systemd made one, where `systemctl --user` finds the user's manager. Run again while that session's Secret Service answers, it keeps the session;
  - on Linux it also installs a root-owned copy of `unshare` at `/usr/local/libexec/goodfetch-test-tools/unshare`, which Collie's PID 1 test runs, and, where AppArmor restricts unprivileged user namespaces (Ubuntu 23.10 and later), loads `ci/apparmor/goodfetch-test-unshare`, which gives them to that copy only (ADR 0049's way for Chrome): no sysctl changes, and `/usr/bin/unshare` stays restricted; and `ci/apparmor/goodfetch-chrome-for-testing`, which gives them to the pinned Chrome for Testing build and its headless shell wherever a test lays them out, so their sandbox starts. It fails unless the copy maps the user to root in a user namespace, and the test fails at once, naming the restriction, when its `unshare` cannot;
  - on macOS it adds the `lo0` aliases, or, on the owner's machine, whose runner user has no sudo, finds them there from `sudo ci/runner/loopback-aliases.sh`, which the owner runs once;
  - on every OS it then runs `xtask os-services check`, which binds each extra loopback address and, on Linux, stores, reads and removes an item through the Secret Service. A service that does not come up fails the job; nothing skips.
- **A test that needs an OS service sits in a module named after it:** `keychain` for the real keychain, `extra_loopback` for an extra loopback address, `tc_netem` for `tc netem` on Linux's loopback interface (`CAP_NET_ADMIN` in the test's network namespace, which CI gives the job's container and, on a runner's own system, `tc` as a file capability). A local `just test` on a host without the service lists it among the parts it could not run and skips those modules' Rust tests with nextest's `--skip`; in CI the same check fails instead, and so it does where `GOODFETCH_OS_SERVICES=required`: `just pre-merge` on a Linux host, which runs those tests itself rather than in `just linux`'s container, runs `ci/os-services.sh setup` first, takes its variables, sets that one and declares `GOODFETCH_TEST_DNS_NAMESPACE=sudo` unless the caller declared a way.
